The binary was compiled on 2 December 2020 at 13:34 UTC, and the config file on the victim's disk was created a little over three hours later. Build and deploy inside one afternoon.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eOur software was installed twenty-one days later. Every date that follows is from the middle of the incident rather than the start, and the first evening the backdoor started on that machine is not something we have any record of.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe last DNS record we hold for \u003ccode\u003ediagrtrack.com\u003c/code\u003e is from March 2021. The registration lapsed that July and nobody renewed it. Nobody re-registered it either: the domain has no whois record today and does not resolve. Even the squatters passed.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe backdoor did not notice. We collected the same WMI subscription from that host three times, in February, August and October 2021, which is how we know the persistence stayed in place rather than being cleaned and reinstalled. When a detection we wrote for this file shape went live in June 2022, it fired on the same machine, and the config was still sitting in \u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFrom the code we know what that machine did for the eleven months after the registration lapsed. The failure changed less than you would think, because the address buffer is never initialised and nothing checks the return value: every five seconds the implant read whatever bytes happened to be on its stack, called \u003ccode\u003einet_addr\u003c/code\u003e on them, and sent an ICMP echo to the result. Then it slept and did it again, until the machine was next rebooted. There was no one on the other end for any of it.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch2\u003eDetection opportunities\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eConfiguration formats do not have to look like configuration formats. A routine that counts spaces is still a parser, and a file containing no suspicious strings, high-entropy blob or encoded text can still carry a C2 address in plain sight. Any pipeline that finds data mainly by looking for things that resemble data risks treating whitespace as empty.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eStructural detections are more durable. A hash covers one binary and a domain block covers one address, but a rule describing the shape of this configuration survives changes to both.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003ePast byte 174, a legitimate \u003ccode\u003edesktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e should not consist entirely of UTF-16 spaces, carriage returns and line feeds. We deployed a detection for that structure in June 2022 and ran it until November. Across our user base, it fired only on the machine we already knew about. It would nevertheless detect another configuration using the same technique with a completely different domain.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe same characteristics also provide several inexpensive hunting opportunities:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eLook for \u003ccode\u003e__EventFilter\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eCommandLineEventConsumer\u003c/code\u003e pairs bound to \u003ccode\u003eWin32_LocalTime\u003c/code\u003e, particularly those launching executables from paths intended to resemble legitimate software.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eInspect \u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\desktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e, especially when it contains the Program Files localization string \u003ccode\u003eshell32.dll,-21781\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTreat a zero-byte \u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\desktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e as potentially significant. That does not tell us how consequential the intrusion was, because we never captured a command, recovered a second-stage payload or established how the machine was first compromised. It does tell us that prevalence alone would have been a poor reason to ignore it.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe implant was custom-built, its configuration format was absent from the rest of our corpus, and searches for related binaries and protocol fragments found no convincing siblings. We assess with moderate confidence that it was deployed selectively, while stopping short of naming an actor or campaign.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBy the time our detection found it, the C2 domain had been expired for eleven months. The backdoor was still starting at 19:50 after every reboot, rebuilding the address from lines of spaces, and trying to contact an operator who was no longer there.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe detection was not wrong. The binary also carries its own compiler receipt in \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003eGCC: (tdm64-1) 4.9.2\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThat is TDM-GCC, a MinGW-w64 distribution, and the linker version of 2.24 and the missing Rich header agree with the string. The PE timestamp reads 2 December 2020, 13:34 UTC. A default build from that toolchain would enter at \u003ccode\u003emainCRTStartup\u003c/code\u003e and import \u003ccode\u003emsvcrt.dll\u003c/code\u003e, so the runtime had to be left out on purpose and the entry point aimed at the author's own function.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eThe polynomial that is not there\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe 14-byte table in \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e is a list of bit positions:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003e0, 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8, 10, 11, 12, 16, 22, 23, 26\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAt runtime the code walks that table and sets one bit per entry, counting down from bit 31:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003efor ( i = 0; i != 14; ++i )\n    poly |= 1 \u0026lt;\u0026lt; (31 - tap_table[i]);\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe result is \u003ccode\u003e0xEDB88320\u003c/code\u003e, the reflected CRC-32 polynomial, which has exactly 14 bits set. Before crediting the author for that: this is zlib. The routine is \u003ccode\u003ecrc32()\u003c/code\u003e line for line, from the null-pointer guard through the pre- and post-inversion to the eight-bytes-at-a-time unrolled loop, compiled with \u003ccode\u003eDYNAMIC_CRC_TABLE\u003c/code\u003e — the zlib build option that generates the lookup table on first use instead of shipping a kilobyte of constants — and GCC has inlined the static \u003ccode\u003emake_crc_table\u003c/code\u003e into it, which is why the table build sits inside the same function. zlib built the polynomial from that list of exponents from January 1996 until the 1.2.12 release, in March 2022, replaced it with a plain \u003ccode\u003e#define POLY 0xedb88320\u003c/code\u003e. The comment sitting directly above the list writes the same polynomial out longhand, as \u003ccode\u003ex^32+x^26+x^23+x^22+x^16+x^12+x^11+x^10+x^8+x^7+x^5+x^4+x^2+x+1\u003c/code\u003e, so the table is the polynomial as a mathematician would state it rather than a constant kept out of a scanner's way. It did not go away for tidiness either: the commit that removed it rewrote the whole routine around the interleaved CRC method of Kadatch and Jenkins, and the new \u003ccode\u003e#define\u003c/code\u003e was a byproduct of chasing a factor of three in speed.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe confirmation is in \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e, which in this binary holds exactly one initialised variable: a \u003ccode\u003edd 1\u003c/code\u003e that the CRC routine clears once the table exists. That is zlib's \u003ccode\u003ecrc_table_empty\u003c/code\u003e. What is not there is the thread-safety flag zlib added in September 2004, so their copy of \u003ccode\u003ecrc32.c\u003c/code\u003e came from some zlib released between 1996 and 2004, at least sixteen years stale by the time they compiled it. The missing constant is a build option, not first URL was already dead.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt did not load a credential form or show a fake Microsoft login page. Instead, it returned an expired tracking response, which is exactly what the operator wanted scanners and analysts to see after the first click.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat could easily have been the end of the investigation: one reported phishing email, a burned link and no visible payload. But the expired URL still exposed enough of the underlying infrastructure to start pulling the thread. What followed led us to Mailer-Go Mission Control, a bulk phishing platform we had not seen publicly documented at the time of analysis.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go handled the delivery side of the campaign, including email templates, recipient management, SMTP infrastructure, proxy support and per-recipient tracking links. The final phishing stage came later, through a Cloudflare Workers page running EvilTokens, a known Microsoft OAuth Device Code phishing kit.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvilTokens itself was not the new part. What caught our attention was the machinery around it: how victims were routed there, how the operator switched infrastructure when a domain was burned, and how much of the delivery chain was designed to disappear once a useful click had already happened.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe link was expired, but the pattern was not\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe reported email contained a tracking URL under:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ecoldchain-distribution[.]com\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe URL followed a structure we later associated with Mailer-Go:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://\u0026lt;8-hex\u0026gt;.link.\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt;/t/v3.\u0026lt;campaign-id\u0026gt;.\u0026lt;recipient-token\u0026gt;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach recipient received a unique token. Once that token was used, later requests no longer reached the live phishing chain and instead returned an expired response. A victim could therefore see the real destination, while a scanner or analyst arriving afterwards saw something very different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat behavior became one of the more useful clues in the investigation. The link was not simply a route to a phishing page. It also acted as a gate that closed after use, limiting what could be recovered later from the same URL.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first domain, coldchain-distribution[.]com, was registered on 2026-06-01. Once detections started catching up, the operator moved activity to healthprofessionals21[.]org, which had been registered the same day. Its tracking subdomains resolved to the same Linode IP used by the earlier campaign, and more than 40 *.link.healthprofessionals21[.]org subdomains were eventually observed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA fake blog on the front door\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAt the root, the replacement domain looked harmless.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehealthprofessionals21[.]org served a technology and gaming blog with articles about consoles, VPNs and multimedia devices. The content was thin and generic, but plausible enough to give scanners and casual visitors something ordinary to index. The phishing infrastructure itself sat underneath *.link.healthprofessionals21[.]org.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/figure_1_healthprofessionals21.png.webp?itok=1Cz8AH2F 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/figure_1_healthprofessionals21.png.webp?itok=6h9fJ0kg 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":878},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/figure_1_healthprofessionals21.png.webp?itok=auBBb2eM 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/figure_1_healthprofessionals21.png.webp?itok=4md6ly0Y 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/figure_1_healthprofessionals21.png.webp?itok=PU4tEhlU 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/figure_1_healthprofessionals21.png.webp?itok=DaQCbWxJ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 1: The root of healthprofessionals21[.]org served a fake technology and gaming blog. The replacement domain initially had little to no reputation-based detection while the phishing chain remained active.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy 2026-06-10, that had started to change. Fortinet marked the domain as phishing and ESET flagged it as suspicious, but the operator had already been using it as failover infrastructure after coldchain-distribution[.]com was burned.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat Mailer-Go is\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go Mission Control presents itself like a commercial email campaign platform. Its admin panel includes modules for campaign management, audience lists, email creation, SMTP providers, proxy routing, tracking, licensing and update notifications.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNone of those features, viewed individually, proves malicious intent. The combination is harder to explain as a normal bulk-mail product.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go supports proxy routing and multiple SMTP providers, and it generates single-use tracking tokens that stop exposing the live destination after the first click. It also includes built-in license management, yet at the time of our analysis we could find no public documentation, GitHub footprint or visible legitimate product presence. The same Mailer-Go login page was exposed across wildcard campaign subdomains.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe assess with moderate confidence that Mailer-Go is purpose-built crimeware rather than a legitimate phishing simulation tool being abused. That assessment is based on the platform as a whole: proxy support, flexible sending infrastructure, token burning, controlled access and the absence of a public legitimate footprint.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUseful fingerprints include:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go Mission Control\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e/assets/js/tracking.js?v=20260522d\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e/assets/js/proxies.js?v=20260513b\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e/assets/js/sending-infrastructure.js?v=20260513b\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e/assets/dist/emailbuilder2/manifest.json\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe observed admin panel HTML had this SHA-256:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e3a026fcb5001327198ac3886e6983183e44b8a5daa0086d34c10cb65130c6f89\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eLive tokens showed the real destination\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe expired links were only part of the picture. URLScan captures of tokens that were still live showed a different path.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eValid Mailer-Go tracking links routed through:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003emysocalledblog[.]com/sev/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat relay then redirected the victim to a Cloudflare Workers page. Following the EvilTokens convention, the Workers subdomain encoded the targeted recipient's email address, which we have redacted:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e9sfo-bnqi-4yap.[recipient-redacted]-s-account.workers[.]dev\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe page presented itself as a OneDrive shared-document flow built around a file named Confidential_Memo.docx. Captures showed several stages of the page, including a “Preparing verification...” screen, a transient “fetch failed” error and, most importantly, the page displaying a Microsoft device code.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=EiTv6k-l 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=kqQcOUui 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":878},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=LPQk8dae 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=kLQ0DqWo 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=xEnob1Sc 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=A8Jt_Ax4 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 2: The final page displays a Microsoft device code and instructs the victim to continue to Microsoft.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/figure_4_od_confidential_memo_copy_code.png.webp?itok=xEnob1Sc","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThere was no fake Microsoft password form. The victim was sent to the real Microsoft device login page. If they entered the code and completed authentication, the attacker-controlled client could receive OAuth tokens in the background.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat has an important consequence for incident response. Resetting the user's password may not be enough if a refresh token has already been issued. We cover the response steps later in the defenders section.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhy Visa appeared in the redirect chain\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eExpired tokens followed another route.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eInstead of reaching the EvilTokens page, they passed through:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003earduinosolutions[.]com/sev/\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eand then landed on legitimate Visa sites such as visa[.]fr, visa[.]com, visa[.]de and others.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat initially creates a confusing picture because Visa appears repeatedly in the redirect data, yet the confirmed phishing lure impersonated Microsoft OneDrive.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe explanation was simpler. Visa acted as fallback cover for dead tokens. Once a tracking token had expired, the user was sent to a legitimate site rather than the phishing page. Someone clicking the link later could reasonably conclude that nothing malicious had happened, and an analyst reconstructing the chain after the fact could reach the same wrong conclusion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe emails were built to survive static scanning\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe identified five phishing EML samples tied to the campaign. Their MIME-header similarity supports the conclusion that they came from the same sending platform.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe messages used generic B2B document themes, including an Autodesk-themed “Bidding Addendum” lure. We found no evidence of detailed industry reconnaissance, so the activity looks more like broad B2B targeting than a tightly tailored campaign.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe email bodies also used a simple obfuscation technique in which characters were interleaved with question marks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eT?h?i?s?\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe text remains readable to a person, but the added characters interfere with keyword matching and some content-based detection systems. At the time of analysis, the observed samples had zero detections across the engines available in our internal dataset.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe also saw Microsoft SafeLinks wrapping in some of the messages. In those cases, the original Mailer-Go URL was stored in the originalsrc attribute rather than the visible href, so analysts reviewing mail traces should inspect both.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat defenders should take from this\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most useful Mailer-Go indicators are likely to come from the delivery layer rather than the lure itself. Phishing content can be changed quickly, and a Workers URL can disappear. The tracking structure is more consistent.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this campaign, Mailer-Go links used *.link.\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt; subdomains and a /t/v3. path containing a numeric campaign ID followed by a long hexadecimal token:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://\u0026lt;8-hex\u0026gt;.link.\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt;/t/v3.\u0026lt;campaign-id\u0026gt;.\u0026lt;recipient-token\u0026gt;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe admin panel also exposed reusable fingerprints, including the title Mailer-Go Mission Control and versioned JavaScript paths such as:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e/assets/js/tracking.js?v=20260522d\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e/assets/js/proxies.js?v=20260513b\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e/assets/dist/emailbuilder2/manifest.json\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor Microsoft 365 environments, a confirmed device-code phishing event should not be handled as ordinary password phishing. If the attacker received a refresh token, resetting the password may leave access intact. Response should include revoking active sessions and refresh tokens, reviewing device-code sign-ins, checking app consent, reviewing registered authentication methods and devices, and looking for new mailbox rules, MFA changes or suspicious file access after the click.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttribution and limits\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe have not linked this campaign to a named threat actor.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operator appears to be a customer of EvilTokens, which is commercially sold. Mailer-Go is less clear. Its licensing features and commercial-style architecture suggest controlled distribution, but we do not know how the platform is sold or shared, and we cannot rule out the possibility that the operator behind this campaign is also involved in its development.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are other limits to what the available evidence can tell us. We observed a low-confidence PE download signal in sandbox telemetry, but no executable was recovered and the same signal appeared when sandboxing the Mailer-Go admin panel itself. Without endpoint evidence showing a PE download or execution after a victim click, we do not treat that as confirmed victim-side malware delivery.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eConclusion\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe reported link was already burned, but it still exposed the campaign’s delivery architecture. Behind the expired URL sat a Mailer-Go tracking server, a backup domain with a fake root site, relay nodes of unconfirmed ownership and an EvilTokens page abusing Microsoft’s device-code flow.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go is the part defenders should track beyond this campaign. The final lure can change. The Workers URL can disappear. The delivery layer leaves stronger patterns: *.link.\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt; subdomains, /t/v3 tracking paths, single-use tokens, exposed admin panels and a small set of reusable JS fingerprints.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSources and credits\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e85ca59061a0c2a3192dac54b484f8035\"\u003eWebsite snapshots (Figures fake invoice in your email is easy to ignore. A fake invoice inside your order history feels different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNorton customers have reported fake Norton invoices appearing inside the Shop app, the shopping and order-tracking app from Shopify. Public reports suggest the same technique is not limited to Norton. Similar suspicious Shop app notifications have used McAfee, Apple gift cards, iPhones, PayPal-style payment claims and other high-value purchases as bait. The impersonated brand may change, but the mechanics are familiar: make the user believe they have been charged, then give them a phone number to call.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat phone number is where the scam really begins.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy the Shop app makes this more believable\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eShop is a legitimate app. People use it to track packages, check receipts and receive order updates. According to Shop’s own documentation, the app can automatically track orders from several sources, including Gmail, Outlook, the email address linked to the user’s Shop account and orders paid through Shop Pay. If Gmail or Outlook is connected, Shop says it scans recent emails for keywords such as “tracking number” and “track your package” to populate deliveries in the Orders tab.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=Bsgcxhe6 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=RisRR8-Z 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":1170},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=eSy-7wue 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=3rbMO4E_ 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":890},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=1cSllT8s 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=DxHsElW1 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":690}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/blog%20image%20screenshot%20%282%29.png.webp?itok=1cSllT8s","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eShop also supports push notifications for delivery updates, payments and favorite products. So the first alert the victim sees may not be an email, an SMS or a calendar invite. It may be a notification from an app they already use for real purchases.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat context matters. The scam text is not especially polished. Some of the examples we reviewed contain awkward wording and obvious signs of fraud. But when the message appears inside an order-tracking app, surrounded by real receipts and shipping updates, users may give it more credibility than it deserves.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe screenshots reported to us show fake orders under generic seller names such as “My Store.” The receipts claim that a Norton or Norton LifeLock subscription has been activated or renewed, usually for several hundred dollars. The phone number appears in the product description, the receipt body or even the shipping address field.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is not how a normal receipt should look.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe brand is interchangeable\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eNorton is the example we saw through customer reports. Norton support now warns that scammers may abuse Android and iOS shopping platforms or applications to generate fake order notifications that appear to be legitimate Norton purchases. Those notifications may include fake order confirmations, fake charges, trusted third-party app delivery and fake support phone numbers embedded in order details.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePublic user reports point to a wider pattern. One Reddit user described receiving a Shop.app message about a transaction they did not recognize, with no follow-up email confirmation, and a message telling them to call support if the order was not placed by them. Another thread includes reports of unrecognized iPhone purchases under “My Store,” suspicious contact emails and users saying they saw no matching charges on their bank cards. Other reports mention fictitious Apple gift card purchases and iPhone orders using the same kind of wording: “If Order Not Placed By You,” followed by a phone number.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe choice of brand is part of the social engineering. A fake security subscription charge plays on two fears at once: being billed for something you did not buy, and being left unprotected if you ignore it. Apple products and gift cards create high-value purchase panic. PayPal-style wording makes users worry about payment account abuse. In each case, the fake order is there to push the victim toward a call.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat we know, and what we do not\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe have not found any evidence that any of the impersonated brands have been compromised.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe also should not claim that Shop or Shopify was breached based on the evidence we have. The exact abuse path still needs confirmation. The fake order may be entering the app through a merchant/order workflow, through email parsing, through account association, or through another legitimate mechanism that scammers have found a way to misuse. Shop’s own documentation shows that orders can populate from multiple sources, so assuming one route would be premature.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat we can say is narrower and stronger: scammers are placing fake purchase claims inside shopping app order experiences, where users are used to seeing real receipts and order updates. Norton’s own support page now describes this class of shopping app scam, and public reports show similar lures using other brands and products.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is enough to warn users without overstating the technical evidence.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eHow the scam works\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe user receives a notification or sees a new order in the Shop app. The order looks like a receipt for something expensive: a security subscription, an Apple gift card, an iPhone, a MacBook or another high-value purchase.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/HowScamWorks.png.webp?itok=pSNPg2B3 receipt says the charge has been processed, the subscription has renewed, or the order is being prepared. Somewhere in the order details, the scammer inserts a phone number, email address or support instruction.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe wording is often clumsy. We have seen phrases like “If Order Not Place By You” and “If Need Help.” Those errors may look obvious when reviewing a screenshot. They are easier to miss when a phone notification tells you that hundreds of dollars have just been charged.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the victim calls, the scam moves off-platform. The person answering may claim to be billing support, Norton support, PayPal support or a cancellation department. The script can vary, but the request usually moves toward credentials, payment data, one-time codes or remote access software.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy that point, the fake receipt has done its job. It has created enough urgency for the victim to enter a conversation controlled by the scammer.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy this is harder for users to judge\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost people understand that email can be spam. They may still fall for phishing, but at least the inbox is a place where scams are expected.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrder-tracking apps are different. Their purpose is to collect receipts, shipping updates and purchase information in one place. A notification from that environment can feel as if it has already passed through some layer of trust, even when the content inside the order is fraudulent.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe have seen the same logic with calendar invite scams. The message itself is not always convincing, but the delivery channel changes how people read it. A fake invoice in an inbox is one thing. A fake invoice inside a calendar reminder or shopping app receipt can feel closer to a real event.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor security products, this also creates a detection problem. The first visible alert may not be an email, SMS or malicious website. It may be a legitimate app notification carrying fraudulent text. The scam still depends on social engineering, but the delivery surface is harder to classify with the usual rules.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat to do if you see one\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eDo not call the number in the receipt.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCheck the charge directly. Open your bank or credit card app. Open the official account for the brand being impersonated. If it claims to be Norton, go to your Norton account or official Norton support. If it claims to involve PayPal, open PayPal directly. Do not use the phone number, email address or link inside the suspicious order.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf there is no matching charge, treat the receipt as a scam attempt.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReport the suspicious store or message in the Shop app if the option is available. Shop’s help pages say suspicious Shop stores can be reported from the store page through the app, and Shopify also tells users to forward phishing messages to its phishing inbox.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNorton users can forward suspicious messages to Norton and should verify unexpected charges only through their Norton account or official Norton support channels.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOpening the receipt does not automatically mean your device was compromised. The risk increases if you call the number, share information, install software or follow instructions from the person on the phone.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat to do if you have already called\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eEnd the call.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared payment details, contact your bank or card provider immediately using the number on your card or inside your banking app. If you gave away a password, change it from a different device if possible. Start with your email account, Apple ID or Google account, banking account and any account connected to the fake invoice.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you installed remote access software, disconnect the device from the internet and remove the software. Run a security scan before using the device again for banking, email or shopping.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you gave a one-time code to the caller, treat the related account as compromised. Change the password, review recovery options and check recent login activity.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe lesson\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eScammers keep testing new places to put old tricks. For years, fake invoices were mostly an email problem. Then similar subscription notices appeared in calendar invites. Now we are seeing fake receipts inside shopping app order histories and push notification flows.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe safest response is to separate the alert from the action. If an app, email, text message or calendar invite says you have been charged, do not use the contact details inside that alert. Go directly to the account or payment provider yourself. Check whether the charge exists. Use official support channels.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fake receipt, the fake support number and the fake emergency are all part of the same controlled environment. The moment you verify the charge somewhere else, the scam loses most of its are constantly evolving, and so are the techniques they use to bypass \u003ca href=\"https://security.googleblog.com/2024/07/improving-security-of-chrome-cookies-on.html\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eApplication-Bound Encryption (ABE)\u003c/a\u003e. In recent weeks, Vidar has been among the most actively developed stealers and, apart from \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/GenThreatLabs/status/2064836539952361976\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003emultiple updates to its string obfuscation\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/GenThreatLabs/status/2061462524604649840\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003ea reworked approach to protecting its configuration\u003c/a\u003e, it has also introduced a novel technique for bypassing ABE. And while there have been many other changes in Vidar lately, with new versions dropping every week, in this blog post we focus solely on the ABE bypass and its technical aspects.\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe ABE Bypass\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, Vidar’s approach to bypassing ABE is somewhat similar to that of Remus/Lumma, in that both try to extract the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly from the browser’s memory. What sets them apart, however, is how they achieve it. Since we’ve already covered the inner workings of ABE in earlier posts (\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eVoidStealer\u003c/a\u003e), we don’t revisit them here and focus instead on the bypass itself. For readers interested in the broader background, we encourage checking those earlier posts.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn short, the end goal of infostealers is to obtain the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e, as it alone is sufficient for decrypting any ABE-protected data tied to a specific application. This key can be obtained in one of two ways: either from disk, where it is protected by two layers of \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectData\u003c/code\u003e (one applied in the context of the logged-in user and one as \u003ccode\u003eNT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM\u003c/code\u003e), or from the browser’s memory – the direction Vidar took.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn memory, however, the key is kept protected with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e using the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. This ensures that even if an attacker manages to read the encrypted form of the key from memory, they cannot simply decrypt it (they would need to invoke the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e function directly from within the browser process). That leaves attackers with two problems to solve: how to find the encrypted key in memory, and how to get it decrypted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eLocating the v20_master_key in Browser’s Memory\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eVidar’s approach to locating the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in memory involves scanning the entire memory of a running browser for a specific pattern. But that first requires a browser process to scan.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the target browser is already running, Vidar reuses the existing process. However, it doesn’t read its memory directly. Instead, it creates a fork of it using \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003eSectionHandle\u003c/code\u003e set to \u003ccode\u003eNULL\u003c/code\u003e, instructing the kernel to clone the address space of the process specified by the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e as a copy-on-write mapping. The resulting process has no threads and is never resumed – it exists purely as a static memory snapshot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo perform the fork, Vidar first opens a handle to the target browser via \u003ccode\u003eOpenProcess\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_QUERY_INFORMATION | PROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS | PROCESS_VM_READ\u003c/code\u003e, falling back to \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e if the initial call fails. It then passes this handle as the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e argument to \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e, requesting \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_ALL_ACCESS\u003c/code\u003e on the resulting child. If the fork fails, Vidar falls back to scanning the live browser process directly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=a0E1a5xW 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=JbjnwDnR 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":303},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=DB6yookn 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=1HwyA59k 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":230},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=8YI9qDxS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=BSGKHXK_ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":179}],"caption":"Figure 1: Vidar forking a browser process. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=8YI9qDxS","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eIf no target browser process is found running, Vidar creates a new isolated desktop via \u003ccode\u003eCreateDesktopA\u003c/code\u003e, assigns it a name of the form \u003ccode\u003ev20_%d\u003c/code\u003e (where \u003ccode\u003e%d\u003c/code\u003e is replaced with the browser’s index in Vidar’s configuration), and launches the target browser in there. In the case of Vidar version 2.1, the browser is launched with the command line \u003ccode\u003e--no-first-run --disable-gpu about:blank\u003c/code\u003e, although the exact command line varies across versions. Since modern browsers use a multi-process architecture, there are typically multiple processes matching a given browser. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_10.jpg.webp?itok=TYAeSWx2","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eIn both cases, the APC routine to be executed is \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e, called with the following three arguments: the address of the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e candidate, its size (32 bytes), and \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e for the \u003ccode\u003eflags\u003c/code\u003e parameter.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSince \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e decrypts the data in place, once the APC executes, the key is decrypted directly in the live browser's memory. To read it, Vidar creates a second fork of the browser process and reads the key from the same address using \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e. It then compares the result against a snapshot of the encrypted key taken from the first fork before the APC call – if the data is unchanged, decryption failed, and Vidar moves on to the next browser process’s candidate.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the data differs, Vidar uses the same second fork to perform an additional verification: it scans the forked process’s memory for the byte sequence \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e and, treating each match as a potentially ABE-encrypted entry (cookie, password), attempts AES-256-GCM decryption with the key candidate using \u003ccode\u003eBCryptDecrypt\u003c/code\u003e (successful GCM tag verification confirms that the key is correct).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhenever the data has changed (indicating that decryption of the key took place), Vidar re-encrypts the key in the browser’s memory by injecting another APC with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e as the routine, preserving the browser’s state. If no candidate produced a successful decryption (i.e., the data remained unchanged after every APC call), Vidar takes a more aggressive approach: it terminates all browser instances, restarts the browser, and repeats the entire scan-decrypt-verify cycle.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy abusing APC injections instead of the more traditional approach of injecting code via \u003ccode\u003eNtWriteVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e that performs the decryption, Vidar trades one suspicious behavior for another. APC injections are arguably less common and may thus slip past some detection systems, though they are still well-known techniques monitored by AV and EDR solutions. But that is the nature of ABE bypasses – each has its own advantages and disadvantages, and that’s exactly why infostealers keep inventing new ones. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicator of Compromise (IoC)\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e459daa809751e73f60fbbe4384a7d1653c36bb06945e4eb3635270924241100a (Vidar
